<dd>Already under an operating lease to an airline. A buyer of an on-lease aircraft acquires a contracted rent stream and a return-condition obligation rather than an empty aircraft, and lenders can underwrite the contracted rent. <span class="cite">Owner: Part VIII §61.</span></dd>
<dt>Operating lease</dt>
<dd>An operating lease is a contract under which the owner (the lessor) rents an aircraft or engine to an operator (the lessee) for a fixed term in exchange for periodic rent, while the lessor keeps title to the asset and keeps the risk that it is worth more or less than expected when it comes back. <span class="cite">Owner: Part II §12.</span></dd>
<dt>Operating versus investing cash flow (inventory versus leasing equipment)</dt>
<dd>Under US GAAP (generally accepted accounting principles) the cash a company spends buying assets it intends to sell is an operating outflow, because those assets are inventory and sit in working capital. The cash it spends buying assets it intends to lease is an investing outflow, because those assets are leasing equipment, capitalised and depreciated over time. <span class="cite">Owner: Part X §81.</span></dd>
<dt>Orange / Lisbon</dt>
<dd>Two 100%-owned maintenance sites listed in the FY2025 10-K. Orange (California) has no retrieved description or date; Lisbon is described in the chain map as 113,000 sq ft and 300-plus modules a year, unverified against a primary source, and was still ramping in July 2026. <span class="cite">Owner: Part VII.</span></dd>
<dt>Overhaul (full disassembly; heavy or full shop visit) (Zero-time (D2))</dt>
<dd>An overhaul disassembles every module to piece parts, inspects, repairs and rebuilds, and replaces the life-limited parts that would fall due within the next run. It is done when the life limits are due, at roughly 20,000 to 25,000 cycles. <span class="cite">Owner: Part I §6.</span></dd>
<dt>Part 145 repair station (Part-145)</dt>
<dd>A Part 145 repair station is a maintenance organisation certificated by the FAA, or its European equivalent under the European Union Aviation Safety Agency (EASA), to perform maintenance and to issue release tags. It is the approval an engine shop or a component repair shop holds. <span class="cite">Owner: Part IV; boxed in Part I §9.</span></dd>
<dt>Part 21 Subpart K (14 CFR Part 21 Subpart K)</dt>
<dd>Part 21 Subpart K is the section of the FAA's certification regulations under which Parts Manufacturer Approvals are issued. <span class="cite">Owner: Part VI §37.</span></dd>
<dt>Part-out / teardown</dt>
<dd>Part-out is the owner's decision to dismantle a retired aircraft or engine and sell its components individually; teardown is the physical disassembly. A run-out CFM56 bought for $0.8–1.2M is reported to yield $1.6–2.2M of used material, net $0.4–0.8M before repair cost (consultancy blog, 2026). <span class="cite">Owner: Part V; boxed in Part I §8.</span></dd>
<dt>Parts Manufacturer Approval (PMA)</dt>
<dd>A Parts Manufacturer Approval is the FAA's combined design-and-production approval, issued under 14 CFR Part 21 Subpart K, that lets a company other than the type-certificate holder legally make and sell a replacement or modification part for a type-certificated aircraft, engine or propeller. <span class="cite">Owner: Part VI §37.</span></dd>
<dt>Peer set (WLFC, AER, AL, HEI, ASLE, SARO, AIR, GE (SEC filers); Safran, MTU, Lufthansa Technik (context))</dt>
<dd>The companies Part XI sets beside FTAI from their own filings: Willis Lease Finance (engine lessor), AerCap and Air Lease (aircraft lessors), HEICO (PMA), AerSale (teardown and used material), StandardAero (independent engine MRO), AAR (distribution and used material) and GE Aerospace (OEM), with Safran, MTU Maintenance and Lufthansa Technik as non-SEC context. They were chosen so that each FTAI activity has a filing that isolates it. <span class="cite">Owner: Part XI.</span></dd>
<dt>Performance restoration (PR; core PR)</dt>
<dd>A performance restoration is a shop visit in which the core is opened and the high-pressure compressor, combustor and high-pressure turbine are restored to recover EGT margin; the fan and low-pressure turbine may be inspected but are not fully disassembled. A core PR is this scope confined to the core module. <span class="cite">Owner: Part I §6.</span></dd>
<dt>Preferred shares (cumulative, perpetual, redeemable; liquidation preference) (Series A, B, C, D)</dt>
<dd>Preferred shares are equity that ranks ahead of the ordinary shares for dividends and in a liquidation and normally carries no vote. FTAI's are cumulative (a missed dividend accrues and must be paid before any ordinary dividend), perpetual (no maturity) and redeemable at the company's option from a first call date at the liquidation preference of $25.00 a share plus accrued dividends. <span class="cite">Owner: Part X §82.</span></dd>
<dt>Prime Engine Accessories (Bristol JV)</dt>
<dd>A 50/50 joint venture with Bauer, Inc. in Bristol, Connecticut, described by FTAI as an MRE repair facility for accessory parts expected to deliver up to $75,000 of average savings per shop visit. It is accounted for under the equity method and was expected to be operational at the end of 2025. <span class="cite">Owner: Part VII.</span></dd>
<dt>Production certificate / production approval (PC)</dt>
<dd>A production certificate is the FAA's approval of a manufacturer's quality system, under which that manufacturer may produce parts to an approved design and release them as airworthy. <span class="cite">Owner: Part VI §37.</span></dd>
<dt>Production rate cap (737 MAX)</dt>
<dd>The production rate cap is the monthly ceiling on 737 output imposed by the FAA after the Alaska Airlines door-plug event of January 2024: 38 aircraft a month, raised to 42 in October 2025, with the review to move to 47 completed in May 2026. <span class="cite">Owner: Part III §21.</span></dd>
<dt>Profit elimination (Intra-entity profit elimination)</dt>
<dd>When a company sells to an entity it accounts for under the equity method, the profit on the sale is not all earned yet from the group's point of view, because the company still owns a share of the buyer and so a share of the asset. <span class="cite">Owner: Part VIII §63.</span></dd>
<dt>Promote / carried interest (Carry)</dt>
<dd>The sponsor's share of a fund's profit above its pro-rata interest, paid once the hurdle is cleared through the distribution waterfall. Carlyle describes its carried interest as "a special residual allocation of profit on third-party capital", generally calculated on realised gains for closed-end funds. <span class="cite">Owner: Part VIII §63.</span></dd>
<dt>Quarantine</dt>
<dd>Quarantine is the segregation of a part whose documentation is suspect, pending investigation. The triggers named in the trade press are mismatched part or serial numbers, unusual formatting on a release tag, and an issuing organisation the buyer does not recognise. <span class="cite">Owner: Part V §31.</span></dd>
<dt>QuickTurn (Miami) / QuickTurn Europe (Rome)</dt>
<dd>QuickTurn is the brand of FTAI's Miami engine business, formed from the assets of iAero Thrust acquired on 4 January 2023 with Unical Aviation as partner; FTAI bought Unical's remaining interest on 1 December 2023 for $30.3 million in cash. <span class="cite">Owner: Part VII; boxed in Part IV §25.</span></dd>
<dt>Reciprocating engine / solid-oxide fuel cell</dt>
<dd>A reciprocating engine is a piston engine. Large natural-gas versions of roughly 1 to 23 MW each are arranged in halls of many units; Wärtsilä describes data-center plants built from 10 to 23 MW engines reaching more than 450 MW per site, with electrical efficiency that "can exceed 50%" and "20–35% less fuel than gas turbines", both vendor claims. <span class="cite">Owner: Part IX §68.</span></dd>
<dt>Related-party transaction</dt>
<dd>A transaction between a company and an entity it controls, manages or significantly influences, which accounting rules require to be disclosed because it is not necessarily on arm's-length terms. Every SCI stream in §63 is one: FTAI sells aircraft to, services, sells engines and modules to, and holds equity in the same partnership. <span class="cite">Owner: Part VIII §64.</span></dd>
<dt>Release tag / serviceable tag (8130-3 or Form 1 tag)</dt>
<dd>The release tag is the signed airworthiness release, on FAA Form 8130-3 or on the European Union Aviation Safety Agency (EASA) Form 1, by which an approved organisation makes a part legally serviceable. It is necessary and not sufficient: the buyer also needs the part's documented history, and because the forms "can be easily generated" buyers vet the supplier too. <span class="cite">Owner: Part V; boxed in Part I §9.</span></dd>
<dt>Repair management</dt>
<dd>Repair management is a service the manufacturer sells to a shop that is not its own: it manages the routing of the shop's piece-part repairs through the manufacturer's repair network and data, so the independent shop buys repair capability rather than building it. GE sold such a contract to Aero Norway, a CFM56 independent. <span class="cite">Owner: Part IV §28.</span></dd>
<dt>Repair vendors / component repairs</dt>
<dd>Component repairs are the restoration of parts that can be repaired rather than scrapped, such as turbine airfoils, cases and seals, by welding, re-coating, machining or re-plating. <span class="cite">Owner: Part IV §29.</span></dd>
<dt>Residual value / residual-value risk</dt>
<dd>Residual value is the lessor's forecast of what the asset will be worth, or fetch in a part-out, at the end of its depreciation life. Residual-value risk is the risk that the realised figure differs. <span class="cite">Owner: Part II §12.</span></dd>
<dt>Retirement rate</dt>
<dd>The retirement rate is the share of a fleet withdrawn from service in a year. GE's planning assumption for CFM56 retirements in 2026 moved from 3–4% of the installed base, to 2–3%, to 1.5–2%; GE reported about 1.5% retired in 2025 and expected about 2% in 2026, and first-quarter 2026 retirements ran "below 1%". <span class="cite">Owner: Part III; boxed in Part I §2.</span></dd>
<dt>Return conditions (Redelivery conditions)</dt>
<dd>Return conditions are the contractual minimum state of a leased engine at redelivery: minimum hours and cycles to the next restoration, minimum cycles remaining on each life-limited part, a borescope and a test-cell run, and records in order. They may exclude parts and repairs that are not the manufacturer's. <span class="cite">Owner: Part II; boxed in Part I §9.</span></dd>
<dt>Revenue and EBITDA per module (derived)</dt>
<dd>Aerospace products revenue divided by modules produced ($2.11M for 2025; $1.94M for Q1 2026; $2.34M for Q2 2026) and segment Adjusted EBITDA divided by modules ($0.89M; $0.82M; $0.84M). A second version divides segment revenue including MRE Contract revenue by modules ($2.96M for Q2 2026). <span class="cite">Owner: Part VII §56.</span></dd>
<dt>Reverse bleed system (RBS)</dt>
<dd>The reverse bleed system is a LEAP cooling arrangement that reduces the need for on-wing fuel-nozzle replacements, which CFM describes as "bringing on-wing maintenance needs in line with CFM56 standards". It received initial engine-level certification for the LEAP-1B alongside the HPT durability kit on 18 July 2026, and was already being deployed on the LEAP-1A. <span class="cite">Owner: Part III §20.</span></dd>
<dt>Revolving credit facility / SOFR (Revolver)</dt>
<dd>A revolving credit facility, or revolver, is a bank line the company may draw and repay repeatedly up to a committed amount. It pays a floating interest rate on drawn balances and a commitment fee on the undrawn portion. <span class="cite">Owner: Part X §82.</span></dd>
<dt>Risk factors / auditor / securities class action / lead plaintiff (Class action)</dt>
<dd>Item 1A of a Form 10-K is the company's own list of the risks it considers material, each with a heading and an explanatory paragraph. The auditor is the independent registered public accounting firm that opines on the financial statements and, for a company of FTAI's size, on internal control over financial reporting. <span class="cite">Owner: Part X §85.</span></dd>
<dt>Rotable</dt>
<dd>A rotable is a part that is repaired and reused rather than consumed: landing gear, the auxiliary power unit, thrust reversers, avionics boxes, flight-control actuators, wheels and brakes. Rotables are the airframe items a teardown sells; consumables and expendables are not worth the paperwork. <span class="cite">Owner: Part V §32.</span></dd>
<dt>Run-out / exhausted engine</dt>
<dd>A run-out or exhausted engine is one with no usable time left before a shop visit: a life-limited part is at its limit, or the temperature margin is gone, or a hard inspection is due. It is the only kind of engine that becomes teardown feedstock, because an engine with life is worth several times its teardown yield as a whole (§32.5). <span class="cite">Owner: Part V §32.</span></dd>
<dt>Russia assets, impairment and insurance recoveries (Contingent cover)</dt>
<dd>In 2022 FTAI "terminated all lease agreements with Russian airlines" and recorded a $120.0M impairment, "net of maintenance deposits, to write-off the entire carrying value of leasing equipment assets that the company did not expect to recover from Ukraine and Russia". At 31 December 2025, 8 aircraft and 17 engines were "still located in Russia" with an insured value of $210.7M. <span class="cite">Owner: Part VIII §59.</span></dd>
<dt>Sale-leaseback</dt>
<dd>A sale-leaseback is a transaction in which an airline sells aircraft it owns to a lessor and leases them straight back, converting owned assets into rent obligations and releasing cash to the airline. <span class="cite">Owner: Part II §17.</span></dd>
<dt>Security deposit</dt>
<dd>A security deposit is cash, or a bank letter of credit, that the lessee posts at signing. The lessor holds it against default and returns it at redelivery if the lessee has performed. <span class="cite">Owner: Part II §12.</span></dd>
<dt>Seed Assets</dt>
<dd>FTAI's term for the on-lease aircraft sold from its own balance sheet to start the 2025 Partnership: 46 aircraft "at an estimated net purchase price of $549 million" in the launch release, about $11.9M each. The 10-Qs attribute the fall in aircraft lease revenue in 2026 to "the sale of Seed Assets to the 2025 Partnership". <span class="cite">Owner: Part VIII §60.</span></dd>
<dt>Segment Adjusted EBITDA</dt>
<dd>FTAI's non-GAAP profit measure reported for each segment: Aerospace Products $160.0M for 2023, $380.6M for 2024 and $671.3M for 2025, and $249.7M in the second quarter of 2026. It includes the profit on MRE Contract revenue (before the equity-method elimination, which runs through a different line) and FTAI's share of the Rome and Bristol ventures. <span class="cite">Owner: Part VII §53.</span></dd>
<dt>Segment margin bases (five)</dt>
<dd>The peers in this Part report profit on five different bases. Segment Adjusted EBITDA (StandardAero and FTAI) is segment profit before depreciation, amortisation and items each company defines as adjustments. <span class="cite">Owner: Part XI §86.</span></dd>
<dt>Senior unsecured notes / bullet / callable (Senior notes)</dt>
<dd>Senior unsecured notes are bonds that rank ahead of equity and subordinated debt in a liquidation but are not backed by specific collateral. They pay a fixed coupon, normally twice a year, and repay their entire principal on the maturity date (a bullet repayment). <span class="cite">Owner: Part X §82.</span></dd>
<dt>Serviceable / unserviceable</dt>
<dd>A part or module is serviceable when an approved organisation has inspected and released it and it is eligible for installation. It is unserviceable when it has been removed pending repair, or scrapped. <span class="cite">Owner: Part I §9.</span></dd>
<dt>Serviceable as removed</dt>
<dd>A removed part is serviceable as removed when it has been inspected to the applicable manual and found within limits. It is tagged and sold without repair. <span class="cite">Owner: Part V §31.</span></dd>
<dt>Servicer</dt>
<dd>The party contracted to manage a vehicle's assets: leasing and remarketing the aircraft, collecting rent and reserves, and overseeing their technical condition, for a fee. In a securitization the lessor that sold the assets is usually the servicer (Willis Lease for its WEST trusts, Part II §17). <span class="cite">Owner: Part VIII §61.</span></dd>
<dt>Servicing fee</dt>
<dd>FTAI's compensation as Servicer of the 2025 Partnership: $10.2M for FY2025, $6.988M for the second quarter of 2026 and $12.849M for the first half of 2026 (so $5.861M for the first quarter, derived). The 10-K describes it only as "customary, market-based compensation"; its rate, its base (committed equity, invested cost, or rents) and the income-statement line it sits on are not disclosed. <span class="cite">Owner: Part VIII §63.</span></dd>
<dt>Shannon Engine Support (SES)</dt>
<dd>Shannon Engine Support is AerCap's 50% engine-leasing joint venture with Safran Aircraft Engines, the Safran half of CFM, that leases CFM engines. It matters to the engine count: AerCap's "over 1,200 engines" at 30 Sep 2025 includes engines owned and managed by SES, while its "470 owned engines" at 31 Dec 2025 does not. <span class="cite">Owner: Part XI §87.</span></dd>
<dt>Shareholders</dt>
<dd>FTAI's largest reported holders per the 2026 proxy: Capital International Investors 13.51%, Capital World Investors 12.43%, The Vanguard Group 10.10% and FMR LLC 5.25%, with nine insiders at about 1.35%. About 102.6M shares are implied. <span class="cite">Owner: Part X.</span></dd>
<dt>Shop visit (SV)</dt>
<dd>A shop visit is any removal of the engine from the aircraft for work in an engine shop, from a visit to fix one finding to a complete rebuild. Part VIII uses one dossier's phrase "teardown and restoration" for the same event; this primer reserves "teardown" for dismantling an engine to sell its parts (Part V §32). <span class="cite">Owner: Part I §6.</span></dd>
<dt>Shop-visit demand (CFM56 shop visits per year)</dt>
<dd>The market's size in visits a year: just above 2,000 CFM56 visits in 2019, and about 2,300 to 2,400 projected for each of 2026 to 2028 with the apex around 2027 to 2028 (GE and Safran). It is driven by the installed base, utilisation and the retirement assumption, which was over-forecast by 1.5 to 2.5 points in 2025 and 2026; Part III §23 carries the forecast curve and Part IV §26 the market reading. <span class="cite">Owner: Part IV.</span></dd>
<dt>Shop-visit forecast / first shop visit (Shop-visit curve)</dt>
<dd>A shop-visit forecast counts the expected engine inductions per year for a type. A first shop visit is the first removal of an engine for shop maintenance since new; first runs are usually the longest, so a fleet's first-visit peak comes years after its delivery peak. <span class="cite">Owner: Part III §23.</span></dd>
<dt>Simple cycle / combined cycle; baseload / backup / peaking; PJM / ERCOT</dt>
<dd>A gas turbine run on its own, with its exhaust heat discarded, is in simple cycle. A combined-cycle plant adds a boiler that recovers the exhaust heat to raise steam for a second, steam-driven turbine, which raises the plant's efficiency but adds years to construction and is only built at frame scale. <span class="cite">Owner: Part IX §68.</span></dd>
<dt>Slot reservation agreement</dt>
<dd>A slot reservation agreement is a paid reservation of a future turbine manufacturing slot, made before a firm order. <span class="cite">Owner: Part IX §71.</span></dd>
<dt>Spare engine / spare ratio (Spare-engine ratio)</dt>
<dd>A spare engine is one held or leased to replace an installed engine while it is in the shop. <span class="cite">Owner: Part II; boxed in Part I §6.</span></dd>
<dt>Special-purpose vehicle (SPV)</dt>
<dd>A special-purpose vehicle is a bankruptcy-remote legal entity that exists only to own a defined pool of assets and the debt secured on them, insulated from the sponsor's other liabilities. Bankruptcy-remote means that if the sponsor fails, the SPV's assets are not caught up in the sponsor's insolvency, so a lender to the SPV looks only to those assets and their leases. <span class="cite">Owner: Part II §17.</span></dd>
<dt>Spin-off / record date</dt>
<dd>A spin-off is a pro-rata distribution of a subsidiary's shares to the parent's shareholders, after which the subsidiary is a separate listed company. The record date is the day on which a shareholder must hold the parent's shares to receive the distribution. <span class="cite">Owner: Part X §77.</span></dd>
<dt>Stand-ready obligation</dt>
<dd>A contractual duty to supply on demand. Under the MRE Contracts FTAI must provide replacement CFM56-5B and -7B engines to the customer as the customer's engines become unserviceable, so it must hold a pool of serviceable engines against the obligation whether or not any particular engine fails in a given period. <span class="cite">Owner: Part VII §48.</span></dd>
<dt>Strategic Capital Initiative (SCI)</dt>
<dd>FTAI's asset-management arm, launched 30 December 2024. It raises third-party capital into closed-end vehicles that buy on-lease, mid-life 737NG and A320ceo aircraft; FTAI manages each vehicle as Servicer, co-invests as a limited partner, and supplies every engine shop visit for the fleet through module and engine exchanges with its Maintenance, Repair and Exchange business (Part VII §46). <span class="cite">Owner: Part VIII §61.</span></dd>
<dt>Stub life</dt>
<dd>Stub life is "the shortest life remaining of all LLPs installed in an engine". In its pejorative sense it is the "several thousand EFCs of remaining LLP life that will not be used and the parts will be scrapped" when life-limited parts are replaced early so that they do not cut short the next run. <span class="cite">Owner: Part I §4.</span></dd>
<dt>Sum of modules / sum of parts</dt>
<dd>Sum of modules is the engine's value computed module by module, or as parts. <span class="cite">Owner: Part I §8.</span></dd>
<dt>Supplemental Type Certificate (STC)</dt>
<dd>A Supplemental Type Certificate is the FAA's approval of a change to a type-certificated design. As a PMA basis, the part is the subject of its own design approval, the STC, and the PMA becomes the production approval for that STC's parts. <span class="cite">Owner: Part VI §37.</span></dd>
<dt>Syndicated / structuring agent / lead arranger</dt>
<dd>A syndicated loan is one shared across many lenders under a single agreement, so no bank carries the whole exposure; the 2026 warehouse is syndicated among 13 banks. The structuring agent designs the deal; the lead arranger assembles the lender group. <span class="cite">Owner: Part VIII §61.</span></dd>
<dt>Technical Implementation Procedures (TIP (Revision 7))</dt>
<dd>The Technical Implementation Procedures are the document by which the FAA and the European Union Aviation Safety Agency (EASA) implement the US–EU Bilateral Aviation Safety Agreement for airworthiness and environmental certification; the current edition is Revision 7 with Amendment 1. Among much else, they set the rules under which EASA accepts FAA Parts Manufacturer Approvals for installation on EASA-certificated or EASA-validated products. <span class="cite">Owner: Part VI §39.</span></dd>
<dt>Test and computation (T&amp;C)</dt>
<dd>Test and computation is the PMA basis an applicant uses when it has none of the manufacturer's design data. It reverse-engineers the part: it measures examples of the manufacturer's part, determines the material and any coatings, and writes its own drawings and process specifications. <span class="cite">Owner: Part VI §37.</span></dd>
<dt>Test cell / test-cell run</dt>
<dd>A test cell is an instrumented enclosure in which a rebuilt engine is run at power to confirm its thrust, EGT margin and vibration before release. Test-cell hours are a physical capacity limit for a shop: FTAI's releases quote capacity in engine tests a year alongside modules (Part VII §49). <span class="cite">Owner: Part I §6.</span></dd>
<dt>Thrust / thrust rating (lbf; -7B24, -7B26, -7B27)</dt>
<dd>Thrust is the engine's forward push, in pounds-force (lbf). A thrust rating is the thrust level at which a given set of hardware is sold; the engine's electronic control limits how hard it may run, and the model suffix gives the rating in thousands of lbf (a -7B24 is rated at 24,000 lbf, a -7B27 at 27,300). <span class="cite">Owner: Part I §1.</span></dd>
<dt>Time on wing</dt>
<dd>Time on wing is the number of cycles or hours between an engine's installation on an aircraft and its removal. It is the smaller of the life-limit clock (§4) and the EGT-margin clock (§5), or, less often, a hard inspection falling due. <span class="cite">Owner: Part I §5.</span></dd>
<dt>Time-and-materials (T&amp;M)</dt>
<dd>Under a time-and-materials arrangement the operator pays for each shop visit as it occurs: parts at the catalogue list price and labour by the hour, with the workscope agreed visit by visit. <span class="cite">Owner: Part IV §28.</span></dd>
<dt>Top-case repair</dt>
<dd>In a top-case repair the high-pressure compressor case is split horizontally and the upper half lifted off, giving access to the compressor blades and vanes for repair or replacement without removing the rotor. StandardAero lists top-case and bottom-case repairs as separate services. <span class="cite">Owner: Part I §6.</span></dd>
<dt>Tranche: senior / mezzanine / equity (Class or Series A, B, C notes; E-certificates)</dt>
<dd>A tranche is one layer of the claims on a securitization, paid in order of seniority. The senior notes (Class or Series A) are paid first, carry the lowest coupon and the highest rating. <span class="cite">Owner: Part II §17.</span></dd>
<dt>Translation table</dt>
<dd>The translation table is Part III's organising device: one row per documented demand driver, stating the aftermarket requirement the driver creates and what it does not stress. "Stress" means that the driver raises the quantity or the price of that requirement; the last column records what the driver leaves untouched or pushes the other way. <span class="cite">Owner: Part III §24.</span></dd>
<dt>TrueChoice (Flight Hour, Overhaul, Material, Transitions)</dt>
<dd>TrueChoice is GE Aerospace's branded menu of CFM56 and LEAP services: Flight Hour (a rate-per-flight-hour agreement), Overhaul (time-and-materials shop visits in GE's network), Material (new and used OEM parts and repairs sold to third-party shops and airlines) and Transitions (green-time leases, exchanges, material buy-back and shorter builds using more used material). <span class="cite">Owner: Part IV; boxed in Part I §9.</span></dd>
<dt>TRUEngine</dt>
<dd>TRUEngine is CFM's designation for an engine whose configuration and material content conform to CFM's own definition: OEM parts and OEM-approved repairs. CFM states that the designation "is being embraced by industry's leading asset valuation providers", so appraisers can treat a non-conforming configuration as a discount (§8). <span class="cite">Owner: Part I §3.</span></dd>
<dt>Turnaround time (TAT)</dt>
<dd>Turnaround time is the calendar time from an engine's induction into a shop to its release. For a full CFM56 overhaul it was about 60 days before the pandemic and 90 to 120 days in 2025; light work takes about 45 days. <span class="cite">Owner: Part IV; boxed in Part I §6.</span></dd>
<dt>Type certificate / type-certificate holder (TC / TCH)</dt>
<dd>A type certificate is the Federal Aviation Administration's (FAA) approval, issued under Title 14 of the Code of Federal Regulations (CFR), of the design of an aircraft, an engine or a propeller, issued after the design has been shown to meet the applicable airworthiness standards. <span class="cite">Owner: Part VI §37.</span></dd>
<dt>Unapproved parts episode (AOG Technics, 2023)</dt>
<dd>In 2023 parts with forged documentation from the supplier AOG Technics were found on CFM56 engines at Delta, United, Southwest and American. They are a different category from PMA parts, which are FAA-approved. <span class="cite">Owner: Part VI.</span></dd>
<dt>Unconditional MRO demand</dt>
<dd>Aviation Week's 2026 forecast gives "unconditional MRO demand of $853bn over ten years". The forecast's own definition was not retrieved. <span class="cite">Owner: Part III §23.</span></dd>
<dt>Up-cycling</dt>
<dd>APOC Aviation's own term for buying an engine in order to tear it down for material. <span class="cite">Owner: Part V.</span></dd>
<dt>Used LLPs (Part-life LLPs; LLP package)</dt>
<dd>Used LLPs are life-limited parts removed from another engine with full records and remaining life, sold as packages with a stated number of cycles remaining and chosen to match the buyer's planned next run. Dealers advertise them as such: "CFM56-7B27E/B1F Engine LLP Package ... <span class="cite">Owner: Part I §4.</span></dd>
<dt>Used serviceable material (USM)</dt>
<dd>Used serviceable material is any aircraft or engine part that has been in service, has been removed, and has been inspected (and repaired if needed) to the manufacturer's manual and released on an airworthiness tag with its full history, so that it is legally fit to be installed again. <span class="cite">Owner: Part V §31.</span></dd>
<dt>USM pricing versus new (USM discount to CLP)</dt>
<dd>Used serviceable material is priced as a percentage of the OEM's catalogue list price (CLP) for the same part: on average 60–80% of a new part's price; 70–75% of list when a specialist engine trader sells repaired material to an airline; up to 85% of list for high-demand parts early in a platform's life; and above list when the manufacturer cannot deliver. <span class="cite">Owner: Part V §34.</span></dd>
<dt>Utilization (FTAI definition) (Utilisation)</dt>
<dd>FTAI's utilization is "the percent of days on-lease in the quarter weighted by the monthly average equity value of aviation leasing equipment, excluding airframes". Each engine counts in proportion to its book equity value, not one-for-one, and airframes are left out. <span class="cite">Owner: Part VIII §58.</span></dd>
<dt>V2500 / IAE (IAE V2500-A5; IAE International Aero Engines)</dt>
<dd>The IAE V2500-A5 is the A320ceo family's alternative engine, a two-shaft modular turbofan built by the IAE consortium, IAE International Aero Engines. <span class="cite">Owner: Part I §10.</span></dd>
<dt>V2500 program (IAE EngineWise agreement)</dt>
<dd>On 6 June 2024 IAE and FTAI signed a five-year EngineWise agreement covering more than 100 full performance-restoration shop visits on V2500 engines in IAE's network, described by RTX as "one of IAE's largest engine maintenance agreements by number of engines with a non-airline customer". <span class="cite">Owner: Part VII; boxed in Part I §10.</span></dd>
<dt>Variable interest entity / primary beneficiary (VIE)</dt>
<dd>Under US GAAP, an entity whose equity holders lack the power to direct its activities, or whose equity is too thin to fund it without support, is a variable interest entity, and the party with the power to direct its most significant activities and exposure to its economics (the primary beneficiary) must consolidate it even without a majority of the votes. <span class="cite">Owner: Part VIII §64.</span></dd>
<dt>Warehouse facility (Warehouse line)</dt>
<dd>A warehouse facility is a revolving bank loan to an SPV, secured on whatever assets the SPV buys during an availability period, with an advance rate and a borrowing base, and with a term after which the balance must be repaid or refinanced, commonly by issuing a securitization. <span class="cite">Owner: Part II §17.</span></dd>
<dt>Waterfall (ABS)</dt>
<dd>The payment waterfall is the ordered allocation of the pool's collections on each payment date: fees and expenses first, then interest on the A notes, then interest on the B notes, then scheduled principal on the A and B notes, then any C notes, with any surplus to the equity. <span class="cite">Owner: Part II §17.</span></dd>
<dt>WestJet transaction / AEI collaboration</dt>
<dd>On 28 September 2026 FTAI announced the acquisition of 27 Boeing 737-700s from WestJet: 17 on lease to WestJet bought by the 2026 SPV in a sale-leaseback, and 10 off-lease aircraft bought by FTAI itself for their CFM56-7B engines and modules, to supply its Maintenance, Repair and Exchange customers. <span class="cite">Owner: Part VIII §67.</span></dd>
<dt>Whole-asset sale (Flight-equipment sale (AerSale))</dt>
<dd>A whole-asset sale is the sale of a complete aircraft or engine from a trader's inventory rather than its disassembly into parts; AerSale's filings call the line "flight equipment sales". AerSale sold 13 engines this way in 2025 against 20 engines and one aircraft in 2024. <span class="cite">Owner: Part V §35.</span></dd>
<dt>Willis Engine Structured Trust (WEST)</dt>
<dd>The Willis Engine Structured Trust deals are Willis Lease's series of rated engine securitizations: WEST VIII, rated in June 2025, raised $596M against 64 assets; WEST IX, rated in December 2025, raised $392.9M against 49 assets. <span class="cite">Owner: Part II §17.</span></dd>
<dt>Workscope</dt>
<dd>The workscope is the specification of what a shop visit will do: which modules are opened, how deep the work goes in each, and which parts are replaced new, repaired, or swapped for used material. It runs from a light quick-turn to a full overhaul. <span class="cite">Owner: Part I §6.</span></dd>
</dl>

## 91. Chain map

The chain map is the one-page picture the orchestrator drew before the dossiers were commissioned, redrawn here from the research file research/chain-map.md. It shows where engines, parts and payments move between the links of the CFM56 aftermarket, and it fixed the segment list the eleven dossiers were written against. The paragraphs after the figure take each box in turn and say which Parts carry it. They say nothing about which boxes matter more; the diagram ranks nothing, and neither does this section.

<figure><img src="chain-map.svg" alt="Chain map"><p class="figcaption">The CFM56 aftermarket chain as the orchestrator mapped it: engine OEMs at the top, engine owners and the capital layer beneath them, shop-visit providers in the middle, the three sources of material below, and FTAI Power as a new sink for cores at the bottom. Green boxes mark FTAI's positions. Arrows show where engines, parts and payments move. Source: research/chain-map.md, drawn 2026-10-03 and corrected for the Rome site (§94).</p></figure>

**Engine OEMs.** The top box is the two manufacturers: CFM International, the joint venture of GE Aerospace and Safran that holds the type certificate for the CFM56 and the LEAP, and IAE, the consortium of Pratt & Whitney, MTU and the Japanese Aero Engines Corporation (JAEC) that holds it for the V2500. New engines are sold with airframes; new spare parts are sold at catalogue list price, revised upward each year; and the OEMs run shop networks and branded service agreements, TrueChoice and EngineWise. Part I §2 introduces CFM and the CFM56 family and Part I §10 the V2500 and the LEAP. Part III §20 carries the new engines' durability record. Part IV §25 carries the OEM shop network and Part IV §28 the OEM aftermarket model, including list prices, escalation and the service brands. Part VI §42 carries the OEM's responses to approved alternative parts, and Part XI §86.2 and §87 carry GE's Commercial Engines & Services segment and Safran's Propulsion figures from their filings.

**Engine owners.** The left-hand box in the second row is the airlines, aircraft lessors and engine lessors that own the engines. They pay for shop visits and maintenance reserves, and they buy, sell and lease engines. Part II §12 to §16 carries the contracts between them: the operating lease, maintenance reserves and return conditions, engine leasing and spare ratios, mid-life economics and green time. Part III §19 carries how an owner decides to retire an aircraft and Part III §22 what lessors and airlines did about extensions, availability and lease rates. Part VIII §58 and §59 carry FTAI's own position as an owner, including Russia, and Part XI §87 carries the three listed lessors' filings (Willis Lease, AerCap and Air Lease).

**Capital layer.** The right-hand box in the second row is the money behind the owners: securitizations, warehouse lines, third-party vehicles, bank debt and preferred shares. Part II §17 carries the instruments from first principles, with the WEST engine securitizations as the sourced template and a worked tranching example. Part VIII §60 to §66 carries FTAI's Strategic Capital Initiative, its two vehicles, their two layers of debt, the Servicer role, the money flows between FTAI and the vehicles and the comparable platforms. Part X §82 carries FTAI's own senior notes, revolver and preferred shares, and Part X §81 the inventory build that the balance sheet funds. The FTAI MRE 2026-1 securitization, priced in May 2026, is carried in Part II §17 and Part VIII §61 to §62.

**Shop-visit providers.** The wide box in the middle is where the engine is opened: the OEM's own shops, the independents (MTU, Lufthansa Technik, StandardAero, ST Engineering, GMF AeroAsia, EgyptAir and others) and the airlines' shops. A visit buys labour, repairs and parts, and life-limited parts and turbine hardware carry most of the parts bill. Part I §6 and §7 carry what a shop visit is and what it costs, with the cost build and a worked example. Part IV §25 to §30 carries the market: who performs visits, how many there are, what limits capacity, where the money in a visit goes and the four options for an engine at its life limit. Part XI §86.3 and §87 carry the independents' margins and statements from their filings.

**FTAI Module Factory.** The green tag inside the shop-visit box is FTAI's position in that link: the exchange of serviceable modules in place of a conventional visit. Part I §3 first boxes the module and the module exchange, and Part II §14 glosses FTAI's Maintenance, Repair and Exchange offer as a variant of shop-visit cover. Part VII §45 to §57 is the full treatment: what the segment sells, how an exchange works, pricing and revenue recognition, the MRE Contract revenue with the Strategic Capital vehicles, volumes and capacity, facilities and partners, the V2500 programme, the segment financials, the short report and the litigation, two worked examples and the guidance. Part X §79 to §81 carries the segment in the consolidated statements, and Part XI §86.7 sets its margin beside the other links on its stated basis.

**OEM new parts.** The left-hand box in the fourth row is the manufacturer's new parts at list price plus handling. Escalation of that list sets the price of every substitute, because used and approved alternative parts are priced as discounts to it. Part I §4 carries the life-limited-part prices by year and derives the escalation rate. Part IV §28 and §29 carry the catalogue, the escalation steps and the slice of a visit's bill they land on. Part III §24 turns escalation into a demand driver (row T-7), and Part XI §86.2 shows what the manufacturer reports earning on the parts and services line.

**PMA parts and DER repairs.** The middle box in the fourth row is the two legal routes to a part or a repair the manufacturer did not sell: a part made under a Parts Manufacturer Approval, by HEICO, Chromalloy and others, and a repair approved by a Designated Engineering Representative. Both are priced as a discount to list. Part I §5 boxes PMA briefly and Part I §9 boxes the DER repair. Part VI §37 to §44 is the full treatment: the four approval bases, critical parts and the hot section, EASA and the bilateral, DER repairs, the PMA market, acceptance by airlines and lessors and the OEM's responses, and a worked parts bill with and without PMA. Part XI §86.4 carries HEICO's Flight Support Group margin. The green tag inside the box, the FTAI–Chromalloy joint venture for hot-section parts, is carried in Part VI §43 (what is filed and what is reported), Part VII §52 (its place in the segment) and Part IV §29 (a first gloss).

**Used serviceable material.** The right-hand box in the fourth row is material recovered from torn-down engines, whose supply follows retirements. Part II §16 carries green time and the trade in part-worn engines. Part V §31 to §36 is the full treatment: what makes a used part serviceable, teardown and part-out economics with a worked 737-800 example, supply and the 2023 to 2026 shortage, pricing against new parts, the players and the module as a unit of trade. Part III §19 carries the retirements that did not happen and Part III §24 the feedstock-scarcity row (T-9). Part XI §86.5 carries AerSale's and AAR's filings, and Part VII §52 carries used material within FTAI's segment, including the exclusive serviceable-parts agreement with AAR.

**New sink for CFM56 cores.** The bottom box is FTAI Power, which converts CFM56 engines into 25 MW generator sets and so removes cores from the aviation pool permanently. Part IX §68 to §74 is the full treatment: the generator set and the aeroderivative from first principles, Mod-1 as FTAI describes it, J&F Power Systems and the $1.465B order, the data-center market and the turbine shortage, competing supply, permitting and interconnection, and two worked examples (implied unit price; cores consumed). Part III §18 glosses Mod-1 as a demand driver and Part III §24 carries it as row T-10. Part VII §49 notes that Mod-1 draws on the same module production as the aftermarket, and Part X §84 carries the 2027 guidance for the Power segment.

**The translation table and the Parts that answer it.** Part III §24 (Exhibit 3.14) turned each documented demand driver into a requirement on the aftermarket, in fourteen numbered rows. Parts IV to XI each open with a paragraph naming the rows they answer. Exhibit 12.1 was compiled by searching those opening paragraphs for the row identifiers; it says which Parts name each row, not which Part treats the underlying requirement, which Part III's Exhibit 3.15 gives.

## 92. Margin pool table

Part XI §86 built the margin pool link by link from the filings and ended with one table (Exhibit 11.4). This section consolidates every margin figure that section carries into one exhibit, with the company, the segment, the fiscal period, the margin, the basis and the source, and then lists FTAI's own margins as Parts VII and X state them. The caveats travel with the figures. The peers report profit on five bases (segment Adjusted EBITDA, segment operating income, adjusted EBIT, GE segment profit and gross margin), which Part XI §86 defines at its head; an EBITDA margin exceeds an EBIT margin for the same business by the depreciation share of revenue, so no two bases can be set beside each other without that share, which is mostly not disclosed. Three fiscal calendars are in use (December, October and May), euro figures are not converted, and "derived" marks a ratio computed in the research from two reported numbers. The rows below follow the order of the chain as Part XI walks it, from the manufacturer to the servicer; they are not ordered by margin, and the exhibit ranks nothing. The shares of a shop visit's bill in Part XI's Exhibit 11.1 (material 60 to 70%, labour 20 to 30%, repairs 10 to 20%) are shares of spending, not margins, and are not repeated here.

Exhibit 12.3 collects FTAI's own margins, which Part VII §53 (Exhibits 7.11 to 7.13) and Part X §79 and §80 (Exhibits 10.8 to 10.13) state. The main series is Aerospace Products segment Adjusted EBITDA divided by segment revenue, where segment revenue is the "Aerospace products revenue" line plus, from 2025, the "MRE Contract revenue" line, a sum the FY2025 10-K's geographic table confirms for 2025. Three caveats travel with the series. MRE Contract revenue is related-party revenue from vehicles in which FTAI holds a 19% stake (20% in the Q3 2025 10-Q), and the profit on it is eliminated through equity-method earnings by a method the filing does not disclose (Part VII §48; Part VIII §63). The segment's cost of sales for FY2025 appears as $1,240,368K in the segment expenses table and the consolidated cost-of-sales line as $1,349,719K, and the gross margins below carry each figure's scope (Part VII §53; reconciliation R-045). And the quarterly figures for Q2 2025 and Q4 2025 are derived from growth rates or a stated margin rather than read from a filing, as their rows say. Consolidated Adjusted EBITDA is the company's own measure, whose exact FY2025 definition and reconciliation the research did not retrieve (Part X §80).

## 93. Company index

The index lists every company, joint venture, investment vehicle and institution named in the eleven Parts: FTAI's own entities and vehicles, the engine and airframe manufacturers, the shops, the parts makers and traders, the lessors and the capital providers, the airlines, the appraisers and data providers, the consultancies and market-research vendors, the regulators and courts, the law firms, auditors and rating agencies, and the trade publications and news services the Parts cite. The index has 224 entries. It was built with a script that searched a curated list of names, each as a pattern allowing for the variants the Parts use, across the eleven Part files, and recorded the numbered section in which each match fell. The entries are alphabetical. Each gives the ticker where the company is listed (tickers are identifiers added for the reader's convenience and are not drawn from the research files), one line on the role the company plays in the primer, and the Parts and sections in which it appears. Where a company appears in more than seven sections of one Part, the entry gives the range and the count. "Unknowns list only" means the name appears in that Part only in its closing list of unknowns. The role lines are descriptive and carry no view of any company.

## 94. Unknowns register

The reconciliation's section D is the consolidated register of what the eleven dossiers could not find: 176 entries, each copied verbatim from its dossier with an id of the form U-D5-07 (dossier D5, its seventh unknown). The reconciliation grouped the entries that ask the same question into 19 clusters covering 44 entries and left the other 132 as singletons. This section reproduces the register in that order: the clusters first (§94.1), then the singletons grouped by the Part that owns them (§94.2), then a table of every entry the orchestrator notes or the Parts resolved in whole or in part during the build, with the resolving fact and its source (§94.3). Every question is given in the words its dossier used, including the dossier's own numbering (U1, U-1) and its internal source references in square brackets, which point to that dossier's source list. Each entry also says which Parts carry it in their closing unknowns lists. An entry that §94.3 lists as resolved is left unchanged in §94.1 or §94.2, so that the register remains the record of what was asked; the resolution sits only in the table. The register is long, and that is its purpose: the primer never fills an absence, so the absences must be listed.

### 94.1 Clusters: entries that ask the same question

The reconciliation found 19 clusters covering 44 of the 176 entries. Each cluster below gives the verbatim question of every entry in it, the dossiers that raised it, what the research already holds, and what would resolve it. The cluster titles and the resolution notes are the reconciliation's; nothing in the quoted entries has been altered.

#### K-01 — Module-exchange pricing

Raised by D2, D3, D5. Rationale, as the reconciliation states it: all three ask what a CFM56 module exchange costs the customer above the LLP life transferred; D5's entry also asks the turnaround, which no other dossier asks.

- **U-D2-07** (D2) — **U7. Module-exchange pricing.** No source gives the exchange fee or a worked price for a CFM56 HPT or core exchange; Safe Fly's "$150,000–$400,000 removal cost per module" is ambiguous. D5 may find it in FTAI's investor materials or the In Practise interview ("FTAI Aviation: CFM56 repair and module swap", URL returned, not read). *Carried in Parts IV, V, VII.*
- **U-D3-20** (D3) — **U20** — Pricing of a module exchange (exchange fee, LLP-life true-up) from any provider, including FTAI. D5 may hold FTAI's own disclosures. *Carried in Parts IV, V, VII.*
- **U-D5-03** (D5) — **U3 — Exchange turnaround time and exchange pricing.** FTAI's quoted turnaround and any published exchange-fee ranges. Trade press (e.g., the In Practise interview [S38]) likely has estimates; not read. *Carried in Parts IV, V, VII.*

**What would resolve it.** FTAI investor materials or the In Practise interviews (located by D2 and D5, not read), or an MRO price catalogue listing exchange options (the SR Technics 2026 catalogue, located, not read). Related, not merged: U-D1-14 (whether an exchange needs a test-cell run).

#### K-02 — Appraiser values for CFM56 engines

Raised by D1, D2, D3, D6. Rationale, as the reconciliation states it: one appraiser engine-value table (IBA, mba or Cirium, with half-life, full-life and by rating) answers every one of these; D6's entry additionally asks for aircraft values, which no engine table gives.

- **U-D1-09** (D1) — **U9** Current half-life base values for -7B and -5B by rating (IBA, mba, Cirium). Resolve with an appraiser publication; this session's searches returned definitions, not values. *Carried in Parts I, II, V.*
- **U-D2-08** (D2) — **U8. Value of a freshly overhauled full-stack CFM56-7B** versus a green-time unit. Needed to close the worked example. Resolve via IBA/Ascend/mba engine value tables. *Carried in Parts IV, V.*
- **U-D3-04** (D3) — **U4** — Any mba Aviation (or Ascend by Cirium) CFM56-5B/-7B value or lease-rate figure. None returned. *Carried in Parts II, V.*
- **U-D3-05** (D3) — **U5** — A current appraiser half-life value for the CFM56-5B (only trend commentary and a trade-press lease-rate range were captured). *Carried in Parts I, II, V.*
- **U-D6-09** (D6) — **U9 — Half-life market values** for 12–15-year-old 737-800 and A320ceo aircraft, and full-life CFM56-7B/-5B/V2500 values, 2023–2026. Resolve: IBA/Cirium/mba published updates (D3 may hold engine values). *Carried in Parts II, V, VIII.*

**What the research holds.** Partly closed: D3 holds IBA's half-life market values for the -7B24 ($5.7M) and -7B27 ($6.4M), Sept 2025; nothing holds a -5B value, a base value, a full-life value or an aircraft value. **What would resolve it.** the IBA Engine Values release in full, mba's CFM56-5B Market Insight (located by D6, not read), or a Cirium Ascend values extract.

#### K-03 — CFM catalogue escalation by year

Raised by D1, D2. Rationale, as the reconciliation states it: both ask for the dated percentage of each CFM spare-parts price revision; D1 asks for LLPs specifically and D2 for the catalogue generally, but the same annual revision sets both.

- **U-D1-10** (D1) — **U10** Years and amounts of each CFM LLP escalation step 2019–2025 (only "Aug 2023, +20–30% on -7B parts" and "~12% LLP escalation" without a year were reached) [33][34]. *Carried in Parts I, IV.*
- **U-D2-01** (D2) — **U1. Catalog escalation 2024, 2025, 2026.** Only the Nov 2022 (double-digit) and Aug 2023 (high-single-digit) increases and Safran's "inflation + 3–4 points" rule were found. Resolve with Aviation Week/Aircraft Commerce coverage of each year's CFM price revision, or GE/Safran earnings call Q&A on "price". *Carried in Parts I, IV.*

**What would resolve it.** Aviation Week or Aircraft Commerce coverage of each year's revision, or GE/Safran call Q&A on price. Related, not merged: U-D11-16 (whose price-increase clause is the same question but which bundles a shop-visit count and a programme-wording question).

#### K-04 — FTAI's disclosure of the Chromalloy joint venture

Raised by D4, D5. Rationale, as the reconciliation states it: both are answered by the same 10-K footnote on the JV (its terms and its equity-method line); the identity question raised by the orchestrator notes (whether the "Advanced Engine Repair JV" is this venture) sits in the same footnote.

- **U-D4-03** (D4) — **U3 — FTAI–Chromalloy JV terms:** formation date, ownership split, accounting (consolidated vs equity method), exclusivity, "at cost" pricing, revenue recognized. Resolve by reading FTAI's FY2018–FY2025 10-Ks for the JV footnote and any 8-K. *Carried in Parts VI, VII.*
- **U-D5-11** (D5) — **U11 — Chromalloy JV contribution in FTAI's numbers**: equity-method earnings line, PMA share of parts, FTAI-stated savings per module. *Carried in Parts VI, VII.*

**What would resolve it.** the investments note in the FY2018–FY2025 10-Ks and any 8-K on the venture. Related, not merged: U-D10-13 (all JV ownership percentages), U-D4-12 (Chromalloy's production capacity).

#### K-05 — A stated PMA discount to OEM list price

Raised by D4, D11. Rationale, as the reconciliation states it: each asks for a disclosed percentage discount of a PMA part to the OEM part; the HEICO portfolio and consolidated-figure questions ride on the same filing read.

- **U-D4-02** (D4) — **U2 — PMA price discount for the Chromalloy HPT blade and vane** (and HEICO's typical discount). Resolve with Chromalloy/FTAI pricing disclosure or MRO price catalogues (the SR Technics 2026 CFM56-7B catalogue may list PMA options [71]). *Carried in Parts VI, VII, XI.*
- **U-D4-09** (D4) — **U9 — HEICO portfolio split** by part category (consumables vs rotating vs hot-section) and HEICO's PMA discount. *Carried in Parts VI, XI.*
- **U-D11-05** (D11) — **U5 — HEICO consolidated FY2025 net sales and operating income; PMA cumulative count; stated discount to OEM; acceptance statements.** Searched [S7, S7a, S8]. Only FSG figures and the 400–550 PMA rate were returned. Resolve: read Item 1 and Item 7 of [S7]. *Carried in Parts VI, XI.*

**What would resolve it.** a Chromalloy or FTAI price statement; HEICO's 10-K Items 1 and 7; an MRO catalogue listing PMA alternatives.

#### K-06 — The WestJet transaction

Raised by D6, D7. Rationale, as the reconciliation states it: both ask what happened to the 27 737-700s (price, lease terms, and where the ten off-lease aircraft sit).

- **U-D6-13** (D6) — **U13 — WestJet transaction terms.** Purchase price, lease term and rent for the 17 leased-back aircraft, the holding entity for the 10 off-lease aircraft, and whether the 10 are booked as leasing equipment or Aerospace Products inventory. Resolve: Q3 2026 10-Q (not yet filed) and D7. *Carried in Part VIII.*
- **U-D7-14** (D7) — **U14 — WestJet.** Disposition of the other 10 of 27 737-700s (balance sheet, part-out, FTAI Power cores). *Carried in Part VIII.*

**What would resolve it.** the Q3 2026 10-Q (not yet filed at the research date).

Raised by D6, D10. Rationale, as the reconciliation states it: one note in the FY2025 10-K answers both; D10 also asks for the subsidiary list and the finance co-issuer, which the same exhibit set covers.

- **U-D6-11** (D6) — **U11 — Equity-method investment** in the Leasing segment: counterparty, ownership, assets, income by year. Resolve: FY2025 10-K "Investments" note. *Carried in Parts VIII, X.*
- **U-D10-13** (D10) — **U-13 — Subsidiaries, JV ownership percentages and equity-method carrying values; the role of FTAI Finance Holdco Ltd.** Resolve with Exhibit 21.1 [S27] and the investments note in [S1]. *Carried in Parts VIII, X.*

**What the research holds.** Partly closed by the orchestrator notes: 2025 Partnership 19% (20% in the Q3 2025 10-Q), $281,740K; Advanced Engine Repair JV 25%, $22,429K; QuickTurn Europe 50%, $9,987K; Prime Engine Accessories 50%, carrying value not returned; Exhibit 21.1 URL located. Still open: which investment the Aviation Leasing segment leases "through"; FTAI Finance Holdco's role.

Raised by D7. Rationale, as the reconciliation states it: both ask for FTAI's share of the Partnership's cash and earnings in 1H 2026; the distribution's amount and FTAI's share appear in both entries.

- **U-D7-07** (D7) — **U7 — Equity-method income.** FTAI's share of Partnership earnings by period; FTAI's share of the 30 Jun 2026 distribution. *Carried in Part VIII.*
- **U-D7-13** (D7) — **U13 — The 30 Jun 2026 distribution.** Amount, source (rent versus sales), FTAI's share. *Carried in Part VIII.*

#### K-09 — Economic terms of FTAI's servicing arrangement with the 2025 Partnership

Raised by D7. Rationale, as the reconciliation states it: both are terms of the same limited-partnership and servicing agreements; D7 itself says "Resolve as U1".

- **U-D7-01** (D7) — **U1 — Management/servicing fee rate and base.** Only "customary, market-based compensation" [S11]. Resolve: the limited partnership agreement or servicing agreement if filed as an exhibit; the 10-K related-party note text; the Q2 2026 10-Q exhibits (ex10.8, ex10.9 were listed in the filing index but not read). *Carried in Part VIII.*
- **U-D7-02** (D7) — **U2 — Promote / carried interest.** Only "contractual profit-sharing arrangement" [S13]. Resolve as U1. *Carried in Part VIII.*

**What would resolve it.** the agreements if filed as exhibits (Q2 2026 10-Q ex10.8 and ex10.9 were listed in the filing index and not read; the orchestrator's gap-fill item 5 was not executed). Related, not merged: U-D7-05 (how aircraft sold into the vehicles are priced).

#### K-10 — Whether FTAI Power is a reportable segment

Raised by D8, D10. Rationale, as the reconciliation states it: identical question.

- **U-D8-07** (D8) — **U7. Segment reporting.** The 10-K FY2025 has two reportable segments [6]; call summaries speak of a "Power segment" for 2027. Whether Power is a third reportable segment from the Q2 2026 10-Q [10], or sits within Aerospace Products until first delivery, was not confirmed. *Carried in Parts IX, X.*
- **U-D10-18** (D10) — **U-18 — Whether FTAI Power is a third reportable segment in the 2026 10-Qs.** Resolve with [S2] segment note. *Carried in Parts IX, X.*

#### K-11 — Status of the securities class action

Raised by D5, D10. Rationale, as the reconciliation states it: the litigation-status question is identical; D10's entry also asks the auditor's name and the internal-control conclusion, which are different questions and remain partly open.

- **U-D5-16** (D5) — **U16 — Securities litigation status** (court, docket, motion to dismiss outcome, any settlement). *Carried in Parts VII, X.*
- **U-D10-16** (D10) — **U-16 — Legal proceedings, auditor and internal control.** Status of the securities class actions filed after the Jan 2025 report and any derivative suits; the auditor's name (recollection, unverified: Ernst & Young LLP); whether any material weakness was disclosed; the content of the Jan 2025 EDGAR correspondence [S12 folder] and the later 2025 8-K [S25]. Resolve with Item 3, Item 9A and the auditor's report in [S1]. *Carried in Parts VII, X.*

**What the research holds.** Partly closed by the orchestrator notes: S.D.N.Y. No. 25-cv-00541, Judge Vargas, class period 23 Jul 2024 to 21 Jan 2025, motion to dismiss filed 20 Nov 2025 and pending (law-firm pages); auditor line with the ambiguity recorded in R-066; internal-control conclusion not returned. **What would resolve it.** Item 3, Item 9A and the auditor's report in the FY2025 10-K.

#### K-12 — Deal terms for FTAI's maintenance facilities

Raised by D5, D10. Rationale, as the reconciliation states it: the dates and prices of LMCES, QuickTurn and the Chromalloy JV are asked in both; D10's entry also asks for the infrastructure businesses' figures and D5's for Orange and Lisbon, which the other does not.

- **U-D10-11** (D10) — **U-11 — Early asset mix and the shift to aviation.** Figures for Jefferson Terminal, Repauno, Transtar and Long Ridge; dates and prices of QuickTurn, the Chromalloy JV and the Lockheed Martin Commercial Engine Solutions acquisition. Resolve with the FY2021 10-K [S15], the FY2022 annual report and the relevant 8-Ks. *Carried in Parts VI, VII, X.*

**What the research holds.** Partly closed by the orchestrator notes: LMCES $170.0M, 9 Sep 2024, 526,000 sq ft; QuickTurn 4 Jan 2023 (with Unical) and 1 Dec 2023 ($30.3M for Unical's interest); Rome $10.5M for 50%, 200,000 sq ft, closed 5 Jun 2025. Still open: Orange (location unsourced, size and date not returned), Lisbon (unverified), the Chromalloy JV's deal terms, and the infrastructure figures. **What would resolve it.** the relevant 8-Ks, the FY2021 10-K and the FY2022 annual report.

#### K-13 — Headcount

Raised by D5, D10. Rationale, as the reconciliation states it: both ask the company's headcount; D5 also asks test cells and by-site counts, D10 also asks the properties list.

- **U-D5-09** (D5) — **U9 — Test-cell count; employees by site and in total.** *Carried in Part VII.*
- **U-D10-17** (D10) — **U-17 — Employees and locations.** Headcount at 31 Dec 2025, by geography; properties list (New York headquarters, Montreal, Miami, Rome NY, Lisbon). Resolve with Item 1 "Human Capital" and Item 2 of [S1]. *Carried in Parts VII, X.*

**What the research holds.** Partly closed by the orchestrator notes: 985 full-time employees and independent contractors at 31 Dec 2025; 494 full-time employees in Canada, about 71% under collective bargaining agreements; test capacity "more than 500" (Sep 2024) and "more than 600" (Feb 2025) engine tests a year. Still open: employees by site; test-cell count; the properties list. **What would resolve it.** Items 1 ("Human Capital") and 2 of the FY2025 10-K.

#### K-14 — Willis Lease's stated lease rate factor

Raised by D6, D11. Rationale, as the reconciliation states it: both ask whether Willis discloses a lease rate factor (D6 also asks FTAI's, which no filing states; D11 also asks the CFM56/LEAP split).

**What the research holds.** Partly closed: D11 derives about 1.0% a month for Willis from the 10-K; D6 derives 0.87–1.1% for FTAI. No stated figure exists. **What would resolve it.** WLFC 10-K FY2025 Items 1 and 7.

- **U-D3-12** (D3) — **U12** — Willis Lease portfolio value, number of engines, and Willis Aero spare-parts revenue for 2025. *Carried in Parts V, XI.*

Raised by D3, D4. Rationale, as the reconciliation states it: both ask what GE's CFM56 used-material or material-solutions offering is and how large; D4's entry also asks for a CFM statement on the Chromalloy blade, which no dossier found.

#### K-17 — Segment revenue and Adjusted EBITDA by year

Raised by D5, D6, D10. Rationale, as the reconciliation states it: all three are answered by the FY2025 10-K's segment tables (Item 7 and the segment note); D6 also asks for each Leasing line and the 2025 quarters, D10 also asks the full statement of operations and the Adjusted EBITDA reconciliation.

- **U-D5-19** (D5) — **U19 — FY2023 segment revenue and Adjusted EBITDA**, and the FY2022 baseline (the segment began ramping in 2022–2023 per the 10-Q language [S10 summary]). *Carried in Parts VII, X.*

Also the FY2021 10-K [S15] and FY2022 annual report (EDGAR index) for 2021–2022. *Carried in Parts VII, X.*

**What the research holds.** Partly closed by the orchestrator notes: the segment table for 2023–2025 (revenues, total Adjusted EBITDA, Aerospace products revenue, MRE Contract revenue, Aerospace Products and Aviation Leasing Adjusted EBITDA, lease income with two readings for 2024) and FY2025 expense lines (operating expenses, G&A, D&A, interest). Still open: 2023 Leasing lines; maintenance and asset-sales lines for 2023–2024; the Adjusted EBITDA reconciliation; net income attributable by year; 1H totals; 2021–2022 with the "70,800" artefact and the R-053 contradiction unresolved. **What would resolve it.** the FY2025 10-K Items 7 and 8 read directly, and the Q4/FY2025 release.

#### K-18 — Capital tied up in module inventory

Raised by D5, D10. Rationale, as the reconciliation states it: both ask how much cash the inventory build consumes (balance-sheet balances in D5; the working-capital and investing lines in D10); D10's entry also asks whether the company defines free cash flow.

- **U-D5-12** (D5) — **U12 — Inventory at 31 Dec 2025 and 30 Jun 2026; segment capex; the cost of building module inventory.** *Carried in Parts VII, X.*
- **U-D10-02** (D10) — **U-2 — Cash flow detail.** Acquisition of leasing equipment, acquisition of property, plant and equipment, proceeds from asset sales, working-capital changes (inventory), and whether the company defines free cash flow. Resolve with the cash flow statement in [S1] and [S2]. *Carried in Parts VII, X, XI.*

**What the research holds.** Known: inventory $1,364.3M at 31 Mar 2026 (D5); operating cash flow −$310.7M (2025), −$188.0M (2024), +$129.0M (2023) (D10). **What would resolve it.** the cash-flow statement and inventory note in the FY2025 10-K and the Q2 2026 10-Q.

#### K-19 — Terms of the January 2026 CFM materials agreement

Raised by D4, D5. Rationale, as the reconciliation states it: identical question about one agreement.

- **U-D4-14** (D4) — **U14 — Terms of the FTAI–CFM Jan 2026 materials agreement** (duration, volumes, pricing, whether it restricts FTAI's PMA use). *Carried in Parts IV, VI, VII.*
- **U-D5-18** (D5) — **U18 — Terms of the CFM materials agreement** mentioned on the Q4 2025 call. *Carried in Parts IV, VII.*

**What the research holds.** Known: the 22 Jan 2026 release's description (OEM replacement parts, thrust performance upgrades, component repair; multi-year). **What would resolve it.** the release body (located, not read) and any 10-Q description.

### 94.2 Singletons, by the Part that owns them

The remaining 132 entries stand alone. They are grouped by the Part whose dossier raised them (D6 entries on leasing mechanics sit under Part II and D6 entries on FTAI's own fleet under Part VIII, following the Parts' own unknowns lists). The note after each entry says which Parts carry it in their closing unknowns lists.

- **U-D8-11** (D8) — **U11. Performance adjustment.** Which metrics the 10% cap applies to (output, heat rate, availability, schedule) and whether it is symmetric (bonus as well as penalty). *Carried in Part IX.*
- **U-D8-12** (D8) — **U12. Service model.** Mod-1 inspection and overhaul intervals; service contract form and pricing; FTAI's expected service revenue per unit. *Carried in Part IX.*
- **U-D8-13** (D8) — **U13. Prior CFM56 stationary derivative.** None found; a definitive statement from GE/CFM or Gas Turbine World's handbook would settle whether Mod-1 is the first. *Carried in Part IX.*
- **U-D8-14** (D8) — **U14. Interconnection queue durations.** PJM and ERCOT queue statistics were not captured; they quantify the "bridging" window ProEnergy's customers describe as 5–7 years [64]. *Carried in Part IX.*
- **U-D8-15** (D8) — **U15. Transcripts.** The Q4 2025 (26 Feb 2026) and Q2 2026 (29 Jul 2026) call transcripts were reached only through third-party summaries [15]–[19]; direct quotations on Power should be taken from the transcripts before publication. *Carried in Part IX.*

#### Part X, FTAI the company (D10): 11 entries

- **U-D10-03** (D10) — **U-3 — Debt detail.** Issue dates and original principal of each note series; principal vs carrying amount; call schedules; covenants; revolver commitment size; any term loans or asset-level debt consolidated from SCI vehicles; total debt and equity at 31 Dec 2025. Resolve with the debt note in [S1] and [S2] and the note-offering 8-Ks. *Carried in Part X.*
- **U-D10-04** (D10) — **U-4 — Preferred detail.** Series B share count, floating spread and reset date; Series C share count, reset terms, redemption date and amount [S20]; Series D issue date and first reset; preferred tickers. Resolve with the equity note in [S1], Exhibit 4.15 to the FY2023 10-K (description of securities) [S25], and the 15 May 2026 release. *Carried in Part X.*
- **U-D10-05** (D10) — **U-5 — Management agreement economics.** The management fee rate and base, the incentive allocation formula, and fees and allocations paid in 2021, 2022, 2023 and Jan–May 2024. Recollection, unverified: a fee of 1.50% per annum of total equity. Resolve with the related-party notes in the FY2021–FY2024 10-Ks [S15][S14][S27 folder]. *Carried in Part X.*
- **U-D10-06** (D10) — **U-6 — Internalization details.** FTAI's closing share price on 28 May 2024 versus the $80.35 implied value; the amount of accrued compensation and reimbursable expenses paid; accounting treatment (expensed as "internalization" cost or capitalized); whether FIG LLC still holds the shares. Resolve with the 8-K exhibits [S10][S11] and the Q2 2024 10-Q [S9]. *Carried in Part X.*
- **U-D10-07** (D10) — **U-7 — Leverage definitions and credit ratings.** The company's leverage covenant definitions; Moody's, S&P and Fitch issuer and note ratings with dates. Not searched (budget exhausted). Resolve with the agencies' press releases and the 10-K liquidity section. *Carried in Part X.*
- **U-D10-08** (D10) — **U-8 — Dividend and share-count history.** Quarterly ordinary dividend 2022–2024 and Q1 2026; basic and diluted weighted shares by year; equity issuances other than the internalization shares; repurchase program terms [S21]. Resolve with [S1] equity note and the Sep 2026 release. *Carried in Part X.*
- **U-D10-09** (D10) — **U-9 — Officers' titles and tenure.** CFO confirmation (Eun Nam), General Counsel's name, David Moreno's current role, start dates. Resolve with the proxy's executive officers section [S6] and the 10-K Item 10. *Carried in Part X.*
- **U-D10-10** (D10) — **U-10 — Governance detail.** Which directors are independent; any remaining Fortress affiliation; lead independent director; bonus metrics and PSU conditions; other NEOs' pay; the "directors and officers as a group" ownership; any surviving Fortress-era arrangement. Resolve with [S6]. *Carried in Part X.*
- **U-D10-12** (D10) — **U-12 — Reasons for the Cayman redomiciliation and the NYSE-to-Nasdaq move.** Resolve with the 2022 DEFM14A [S17] and the listing-transfer 8-K. *Carried in Part X.*
- **U-D10-14** (D10) — **U-14 — 2025 guidance as set on 30 Dec 2024 and the Feb 2026 2026 guidance in full (including FTAI Power and consolidated).** Resolve with [S24] and [S3]. *Carried in Part X.*
- **U-D10-15** (D10) — **U-15 — Risk factors in full.** Supplier dependence on CFM/GE and other OEMs for new parts and repairs, regulatory approvals (FAA/EASA, PMA), SCI vehicle risks, key personnel, litigation; annual customer concentration and customer names. Only Russia, leverage, SCI, customer concentration (Q3 2025) and MRE stand-ready obligations were confirmed. Resolve with Item 1A of [S1]. *Carried in Part X.*

The orchestrator ran a small number of its own searches after the dossiers were written, recorded in research/orchestrator-notes.md, and the Parts' authors found a few further facts while writing. Exhibit 12.5 lists every register entry those efforts resolved, in whole or in part, with the resolving fact and its source. "Part" in the extent column means the entry still has open components, which the row names. The entries themselves stand unaltered above; a reader checking a question should read the entry there and the resolution here.

## 95. Consolidated sources

Each Part ends with a numbered list of the sources it used. This section merges the eleven lists into one. The merge was done by a script: it read every numbered entry in each Part's "Sources" section, keyed entries by their URL (or by title where an entry has no URL, after normalising case and punctuation), kept one entry per key with the shortest of the descriptions the Parts gave it, sorted the result by publisher and then by date, numbered it, and recorded which Parts cite each entry. The eleven lists held 849 citations of external sources; after the merge there are 442 distinct sources. Entries that point to the primer's own research files rather than to an external document are listed separately in §95.2, so that the numbered list contains only external sources. The numbering here is this section's own and does not match the numbers inside the Parts, which each Part's inline citations use. Where an entry gives two URLs, the Part cited both forms of the same document (for example an SEC exhibit and the GlobeNewswire release). Publisher names were normalised only where the same publisher appeared under two spellings; the descriptions are otherwise as the Parts wrote them, including notes such as "located, not read" and "date not visible".

### 95.3 Sources and basis

The research for this primer is as of 3 October 2026. Every figure carries its source and date where the Parts state them, and every disagreement between sources is presented with both figures; nothing is averaged and nothing is chosen. The sources fall into a small number of kinds, and the Parts label each. Filings with the US Securities and Exchange Commission (Forms 10-K, 10-Q, 8-K, 20-F, 6-K, DEF 14A and their exhibits) were reached through search restricted to sec.gov, because direct access to EDGAR was blocked in the build environment; each filing figure is cited to the EDGAR URL the search returned, and where a search returned two readings of one line (for example FTAI's 2024 lease income) both are carried. Company releases were reached on GlobeNewswire, PR Newswire or the company's investor-relations pages. Earnings-call content was reached through third-party coverage (GuruFocus, MarketBeat, Quartr, Nasdaq.com, Barchart and transcript services) where the Parts say "call coverage", and the figures that rest on it alone, such as the 1,200-module 2026 target and the $88.2M Q2 2026 Leasing Adjusted EBITDA, are labelled so wherever they appear. Trade press (Aviation Week, Leeham News, Aircraft Commerce, Cargo Facts, AviTrader and others), regulators' documents (FAA, EASA, the eCFR) and appraisers' publications (IBA, mba Aviation, Cirium) are cited by name and date. Consultancy blogs, above all Safe Fly Aviation, are labelled "consultancy blog" at every use, and market-research vendors' market sizes are labelled as vendor figures whose scope definitions were not retrieved. A few entries are marked "located, not read" or "title located; body not read": the document's existence and title were established but its text was not retrieved, and the Parts use only the title. The eleven dossiers (D1 to D11), the reconciliation and the term registry from which the Parts were written are published alongside under research/, with the orchestrator notes and the chain map.

## Build notes

The primer was built from eleven research dossiers (D1 to D11 in research/dossiers/), one per segment of the chain map, each written by a separate research agent against a common brief; from a reconciliation pass (research/reconciliation.md) that cross-checked the dossiers and produced the tensions, corroborations, information-needs and unknowns registers and the binding instructions to writers; from a term registry (research/term-registry.md) that assigned every term to an owner Part; and from an outline (outline.md) that fixed the section numbers used throughout. Facts the orchestrator gathered after the dossiers were written sit in research/orchestrator-notes.md. The Spine was written last and placed first.

The render pipeline is in build/. Each Part is one markdown file in parts/ with a small block of metadata at the top (title, running header, date line, in the YAML key-and-value format), a part-divider block and the components the writer brief defines (definition boxes, worked examples, callouts and exhibits as blocks of HTML, the web page markup language, with markdown inside). build/render.py converts a Part with Python-Markdown (tables, definition lists, footnotes, attribute lists, a table of contents and markdown-in-HTML) and paginates it with WeasyPrint under build/assets/primer.css, an EB Garamond stylesheet with running headers, a page-numbered contents list and tables that flow across pages. build/validate.py checks each rendered PDF for near-blank pages, exhibits without tables and unbalanced components, and reports page and word counts. build/compile_master.sh renders the cover, the Spine and the twelve Parts into one PDF. This Part's compiled sections were generated by a script that reads the registry, the Part files and the reconciliation, and fills a template; the script is reproducible and was run on 2026-10-03.

Three limits are known and should be read with the primer. First, there is no staged corpus: the research environment could not download filings or documents into a local store, so every figure was read through search results and is cited to the URL the search returned, and some documents were located by title but never read. Second, the research agents shared a pool of 200 web searches that was exhausted during the first wave; dossier D5 (Aerospace Products, the deepest Part) and dossier D10 (the company) were affected most, and the orchestrator's gap-fill queue for them was only partly executed (§94.3 lists what was and was not closed). Third, several figures rest on third-party coverage of earnings calls rather than on filings or releases; the Parts label every such figure "call coverage", and §95.3 names the two that matter most. Within those limits the primer is descriptive and complete as to what was found, and §94 is complete as to what was not.
